of the Benedictine Order

# **T R E A T I S E,**

# **On the Great Stone**

->of the Ancients / upon which so many thousands<-
->of Masters from the beginning of the world<-
->have labored:<-

### **I. Together with his own**
clear repetition and brief recap / in which the true Light of the Sages is placed before the eyes in the Philosophical manner / Together with a report on the most prominent minerals and their properties.

### **II. Concerning the Microcosm** <note>original Latin: "De Microcosmo"</note>**, or the Little**
World of Man.

### **III. On the Great Secret of the World /**
**and its Medicine /** pertaining to mankind / etc.
->Highly necessary to be known by all those<-
->who love the foundation of Wisdom.<-

### **IV. On the Knowledge and Hidden**
**Secrets of the Seven Planets.**

**Published for the benefit of the sons of doctrine** <note>original Latin: "Filiis doctrinæ"; a term used for students or initiates of alchemy</note> / **and now**
brought to light quite anew through printing, with its associated figures <note>referring to the woodcut illustrations or emblems</note> /

->By<-

**Johann Thölde, of Hesse.** <note>Johann Thölde was a chemist and salt-maker who is often suspected of being the actual author behind the pseudonym 'Basilius Valentinus.'</note>

->In the Year 1626.<-

<summary>This is the title page for a 1626 edition of a famous alchemical text attributed to the legendary Benedictine monk Basilius Valentinus, covering the Philosopher's Stone, human nature, and planetary secrets. It was edited and published by Johann Thölde, a figure central to the Rosicrucian and alchemical movements of the early 17th century.</summary>

## TO THE READER <note>original Latin: "AD LECTOREM"</note>

->My dear reader, whoever you are,<-
->Judge not this book before you read it,<-
->Lest you, by mere appearance alone,<-
->Judge that which is written herein;<-
->But first take heed within your mind<-
->To what each word contains within itself;<-
->Thus you will wisely judge rightly<-
->Who is a Bishop <term>Bischoff; used here metaphorically to represent a master or high authority of the art</term> or a barber’s apprentice <term>Badersknecht; a lowly, unskilled worker; in this context, a "clumsy" pretender to alchemy</term>.<-
->No judgment is recognized at the end<-
->Until justice has finished its course.<-

---

<image-desc>An ornamental divider consisting of a horizontal row of linked oval motifs.</image-desc>

> Be subject to God, performing your assigned duty, <note>original Latin: "Subditus esto Deo, mandato munere fungens"</note>
> And in wretched circumstances, hope and seek for help. <note>original Latin: "Et spera in miseris, & pete, rebus, opem"</note>

# EPISTLE

worthy of no little praise / who, as our ancestors lived / and recorded all kinds of circumstances / as the state of the world was in their time for our benefit / composed these in writings / and left them behind for everlasting memory / and bequeathed them to us / who otherwise / had this not been done or written down by them / would know nothing at all of it / and thus, through ignorance, many memorable histories and deeds / many arts and wonders / which are preserved through such means / would have had to sink and perish.

In the beginning, however, it was prescribed for us / and left to us through God’s counsel / through His holy Word in the Bible / through Moses / the holy Prophets and Apostles / how our first parents were created into the world / how they fell into sin through the deception of the old serpent and dragon, the Devil <note>A reference to the Book of Genesis and Revelation, common in 17th-century theological framing of history.</note> / how the entire world thereafter until Noah and his family / together with every kind of animal / was again exterminated and drowned by the Great Flood /

Thus also in worldly arrangements has the highly enlightened Emperor <note>original: *Imperator*</note> Justinian and others prescribed for us certain laws, civil codes <note>original: *Policey*; referring here to the general regulation and "policing" of a community</note>, order, and standards for how we should live among one another, govern, and have decisions in all cases; which worldly laws and rights are still strictly held by the peoples of these lands, and they can by no means do without them.

In like manner, other secular historians <note>original: *Historici*</note> have left behind their praise through writings for the benefit of us descendants, so that we too may have experience of what kind of life, order, and essence the ancients led before us. Josephus <note>Flavius Josephus (c. 37–100 AD), a Romano-Jewish historian essential for understanding the Jewish-Roman wars.</note> described the wars and histories of the Jews. Titus Livius <note>Livy (c. 59 BC – 17 AD), author of a monumental history of Rome.</note> wrote of how the Romans contested and conquered <note>original: *recuperirt*; implying the recovery or acquisition of territory</note> almost the whole world; and even if he had recorded nothing more, the single history of the Romans and those of Carthage would be worthy enough of praise—to say nothing of many others—from which one can read of the state of the world: how it rose and flourished in glory <note>original: *Herrligkeit*</note>, only to sink back into ruin soon after.

Imperator*</note> achieved such glorious **victories** <note>original: *victorias*</note> and performed notable deeds; as well as what other noble **Electors** <note>original: *Churfürsten*; the high-ranking princes of the Holy Roman Empire who elected the Emperor</note>, Princes, and Potentates of the German nation achieved at that time and endured in times of war and peace; which indeed **Johannes Sleidanus** <note>A prominent diplomat and historian (1506–1556) whose work *De statu religionis et reipublicae* was the standard history of the Reformation era.</note> has composed into a certain order, and for which he is worthy of gratitude.

**Münster** <note>original: *Munsterus*; referring to Sebastian Münster (1488–1552), author of the *Cosmographia*, the earliest German-language description of the world.</note> presented a model for describing the whole world; just as now those who endeavor to thoroughly record the past wars and circumstances that have occurred in **France** <note>original: *Gallia*</note> as well as in **the Netherlands** <note>original: *Belgica*; referring to the Low Countries during the period of political and religious upheaval known as the Eighty Years' War</note>—which have continued until now and have not yet been brought to an end—are equally to be praised.

# EPISTLE

What would we know of **medicine** <note>original: *Medicin*</note> and **the healing arts** <note>original: *Artzney*; in this period, this referred broadly to both the study of medicine and the preparation of remedies.</note> if the ancients, through many kinds of long-lasting **experimentation** <note>original: *Experientz*</note> and experience, had not brought such things to light and left them to us through the writing down of their recipes and preparations? Likewise, the **philosophers** <note>original: *Philosophi*; referring here to "natural philosophers," the precursors to modern scientists.</note> have also expanded the light of day through the investigation and deep exploration of their diligent **contemplation** <note>original: *Speculation*; meaning theoretical study or deep intellectual inquiry.</note>, so that all arts, and even all crafts, have now been brought so high and have ascended so far that one must almost say they could not be advanced any further.

Do we not find magnificent people experienced and practiced in all kinds of languages? Such skill was previously a **rare prize** <note>original: *thewr Wildpret*; literally "expensive venison," an idiom for something rare and highly valued.</note>, for he who is not now knowledgeable in several languages is almost no longer fit for the courts of lords. How also have the **astronomers** <note>original: *Astronomi*</note> and **mathematicians** <note>original: *Mathematici*</note> reached such heights that I believe, if **Ptolemy** <note>original: *Ptolomæus*; the 2nd-century Greco-Roman astronomer whose geocentric model dominated scientific thought for over a millennium.</note> were alive now, he would be amazed and almost unable to find his way, [considering] what the Serene, High-born Prince and Lord, **Lord William, Landgrave of Hesse** <note>referring to William IV (1532–1592), a pioneer in astronomical observation who established the first major modern observatory in Europe at Kassel.</note>, of highly-praiseworthy and blessed **memory—**

<meta>catchword: —brance [niß/]</meta>

<summary>This page argues that modern progress in medicine, linguistics, and astronomy is only possible because previous generations recorded their findings, specifically praising the astronomical contributions of William IV of Hesse.</summary>

->A v<-

...attainments achieved in **astronomy** <note>original: *Astronomia*</note>, or those which **Tycho Brahe** <note>A celebrated Danish nobleman and astronomer (1546–1601) known for his accurate and comprehensive astronomical observations.</note> of **Denmark** <note>original: *Dennemarck*</note> has put **into practice** <note>original: *in usu*</note>. How the orators of this time strive for elegant words in both the Latin and German languages! The **poets** <note>original: *Poëtē*</note> in their heroic **verses** <note>original: *Carminibus*</note>, and the **musicians** <note>original: *Musici*</note> in the composition of lovely and pleasant melodies. The **philosophers** <note>original: *Philosophi*</note>, through keen **contemplation** <note>original: *speculation*; here meaning deep intellectual study</note> and the **dissection** <note>original: *Zerlegung*</note> of all **bodies** <note>original: *Corporum*</note>, strive to fathom and investigate every foundation and property of nature. 

Just as the **physicians** <note>original: *Medici*</note> of both internal and external **medicine** <note>original: *Medicin*</note> apply more diligence day after day to fathom those things which they did not know before and which were hidden from their eyes. This has been found in the esteemed **philosopher** <note>original: *Philosopho*</note> **Theophrastus Paracelsus** <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), a Swiss physician and alchemist who pioneered the use of chemicals and minerals in medicine.</note>, whose legacy of invincible books provides sufficient testimony, and which **by the very deed** <note>original: *in ipso facto*</note> prove themselves worthy of praise through their proper preparation and **administration** <note>original: *Administrirung*</note>.

Since, then, most gracious **Prince-Elector** <note>original: *Churfürst*; one of the high-ranking princes of the Holy Roman Empire who had the right to elect the Emperor.</note> and Lord, as well as most gracious **Electress** <note>original: *Churfürstin*</note> and Lady, this book by **Brother Basilius** <note>original: *Fratris Basilii*; referring to Basilius Valentinus, the purported Benedictine monk and alchemist whose works are being presented here.</note> [Valentinus]...

### EPISTOLA
<note>Latin: "Letter" or "Epistle." This functions here as a formal introductory heading for the dedication or preface.</note>

...[the writings of] **Basilius Valentinus** <note>original: *Valentini*; a legendary alchemical figure traditionally described as a 15th-century Benedictine monk, though his works first appeared in the early 1600s.</note> of the **Benedictine Order** <note>original: *Benedicter Ordens*</note>, written by him many years ago, have come into my hands. This work **philosophizes** grandly about the ancient **Philosopher’s Stone** <note>original: *Stein der Weisen*; the legendary "tincture" or substance sought by alchemists to transmute base metals into gold and create the Elixir of Life.</note>, providing a clear **recapitulation** <note>original: *Repetition*; a summary or instructional review</note> of the subject. 

Likewise, it treats the most noble **minerals** <note>original: *Mineralibus*</note> and **salts** <note>original: *Saltzen*</note>, and also discusses **lime** <note>original: *Kalche*</note>, **vinegar** <note>original: *Essig*</note>, and **wine** <note>original: *Weine*</note>, alongside a special report **on the Microcosm** <note>original: *de Microcosmo*; "the little world," a philosophical concept viewing the human being as a miniature reflection of the entire universe.</note>, or the little world of Man. Similarly, regarding the great mystery of the world and its **medicine** <note>original: *Artzney*</note>, as well as the mastery and hidden secrets of the **seven planets** <note>The "planets" of traditional alchemy: Saturn (lead), Jupiter (tin), Mars (iron), Sun (gold), Venus (copper), Mercury (quicksilver), and Moon (silver).</note>, I have previously published a portion as the first **treatise** <note>original: *Tractatum*</note>, together with the **Twelve Keys** <note>original: *12. Schlüsseln*; a series of twelve symbolic steps or "keys" describing the alchemical process to create the Philosopher's Stone.</note>, and had it printed at my own expense. 

However, that work was soon **corrupted** <note>original: *corrumpirt*</note>, falsified, and mutilated by a **pirated edition** <note>original: *Nachdruck*; literally a "reprint," but here referring to an unauthorized or poor-quality copy.</note>. Since many honor-loving, noble people have repeatedly requested that this published treatise—which they read with particular pleasure—be fully brought to print, so that this **philosophical material** <note>original: *Philosophische Materia*</note> might be completed, and through accidental, unforeseen—

<summary>The author introduces the works of the alchemist Basilius Valentinus, noting his own previous attempts to publish them which were undermined by poor-quality pirated editions.</summary>
<keywords>Basilius Valentinus, Benedictine Order, Philosopher's Stone, minerals, salts, Microcosm, Seven Planets, Twelve Keys, printing history, alchemy</keywords>

## DEDICATION II
<note>Latin: *Dedicatoria*. This marks the second part of the formal introductory dedication.</note>

...[by] insignificant means, <note>Completing the word "geringliche" from the previous page's catchword.</note> I have finally consented, out of compelling motives, to comply with them. I have also brought this same work to paper through public publication, as is now fully evident to all. It is my hope that—since this book will not be the least esteemed among other **philosophical writings** <note>original: *Philosophischen Schrifften*; in this era, "philosophy" specifically referred to natural philosophy and alchemy.</note>, but will indeed be counted and judged by the wise among the most prominent of such works—it will serve many **lovers of the arts** <note>original: *Kunstliebenden*; refers to students of the "Hermetic Art" or alchemy.</note> as a special pleasure and for better **reflection**, as well as for their benefit and welfare. This is especially true since one will hardly find another who has described this **subject matter** <note>original: *Materia*</note> more clearly and brightly until now.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Now, so that such a book and incorporated **treatise** <note>original: *Tractat*</note> might have a certain distinguished protection—and mentioning it without boasting—**Your Electoral Graces** <note>original: *Ewer Churf. G. G.* (Euer Churfürstliche Gnaden); a title for the high-ranking Prince-Electors of the Holy Roman Empire.</note> have both applied excellent and notable diligence to the promotion of all manner of **liberal arts**. Therefore, I have [presented] to Your Electoral Graces this book, which in plain sight...

# DEDICATORY EPISTLE
<note>Latin: *Epistola Dedicatoria*. This is the formal letter of dedication that traditionally precedes the main text of an early modern book.</note>

...[perceived as] **insignificant**, <note>Completing the sentence from the previous page's catchword "gering-".</note> yet according to its true meaning shall be found important. As to a distinguished **potentate** <note>A monarch or ruler with great power.</note> and head of the **Holy Empire of the German Nation** <note>Refers to the Holy Roman Empire of the German Nation.</note>, I wish to ascribe and dedicate [this work]. Humbly praying that **Your Electoral Grace** <note>original: *E. Churf. G. G.* (Euer Churfürstliche Gnaden); a high-ranking title for the princes who elected the Emperor.</note> will graciously accept this from my lowly and humble person, serving as this book’s faithful protector; and that Your Electoral Grace might likewise keep me recommended to your favor. I wish Your Electoral Grace God’s blessing, a long life with good health, together with Your Electoral Grace’s young lords and ladies, as well as a prosperous reign and both temporal and eternal **welfare**. <note>original: *Wolfahrt*; meaning well-being or prosperity.</note> Given at **Frankenhausen**, <note>A town in Thuringia, Germany, where the author was involved in the salt industry.</note> the 4th of January, in the year 1602.

->Your Electoral Grace's<-

->humble servant,<-

->**Johann Thölde**.<- <note>Johann Thölde (c. 1565–1614) was a chemist and salt-works official. He is most famous for publishing the alchemical works of Basilius Valentinus, though many scholars believe Thölde himself may have been the true author of those texts.</note>

# PREFACE.
## By Brother <note>original Latin: *Fratris*.</note> Basilius Valentinus <note>Basilius Valentinus is the legendary author of numerous alchemical texts. While tradition describes him as a 15th-century Benedictine monk, many historians believe the name was a pseudonym used by the editor Johann Thölde.</note> of the Bene-
## dictine Order, concerning the Great Stone
## of the Ancient Sages.

When human fear took hold of me, I began to consider—out of **nature’s frailty**—the misery of this world; and I wept inwardly for the **transgression** <note>original: *Missethat*; referring to the Original Sin of Adam and Eve.</note> committed by our first parents, and that no **repentance** <note>original: *Bußfertigkeit*.</note> walked upon the earth, but instead mankind became ever more wicked. Furthermore, [considering] that the eternity appointed for the punishment of the unrepentant was unfathomable and without end, I hurried as much as I ever could to escape evil, to bid the world good night, <note>A common early modern idiom for retiring from secular life to enter a monastery.</note> and to pledge myself as a servant to God.

Once I had been in my Order for some time, I wished that my remaining daily work—after completing the goal I had set for my prayers—should not pass in vain, and that my thoughts might not give cause for new sins through **idleness**. <note>The author adheres to the monastic tradition that "idleness is the enemy of the soul," choosing to study the secrets of God's creation as a form of productive labor.</note> Therefore, I resolved to [examine] nature from one—

<meta>catchword: another</meta>

<summary>In this preface, the author Basilius Valentinus explains his spiritual journey, citing the wickedness of the world and the fear of eternal punishment as his reasons for joining the Benedictine Order and studying the secrets of nature.</summary>
<keywords>Basilius Valentinus, Benedictine Order, Philosopher's Stone, Original Sin, repentance, idleness, monastic life, alchemy</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Preface" of Basilius Valentinus. The previous page ended with the author deciding to study nature from one perspective to another; this page details his transition from theoretical study of monastic books to practical alchemical experimentation, prompted by a fellow monk's illness.</meta>

# PREFACE.

...another [perspective] / and through such dissection <note>original: *Zerlegung*; essentially an alchemical analysis where a substance is "laid apart" into its constituent elements.</note>
to investigate its secrets / which I then found to be the
highest pursuit in temporal things, second only to the eternal.
And because I found many books in our monastery,
which had been written long ago by the wise masters before me /
who had also fathomed Nature through their study
and exploration / it gave me a more willing spirit
to also learn what they had known.
And although it was a heavy burden for me to take in hand /
since all things are easier in the end /
and difficult in the beginning /
nonetheless God granted to me—because I prayed to Him diligently—
what others before me had seen.

In our monastery I had a **fellow brother**, /
whom the **tearing stone** <note>original: *reissende Stein*; a period term for kidney or bladder stones, referring to the "tearing" or "gripping" pain of the condition.</note> made very sick /
and he often lay bedridden. / He had sought out many
**physicians** <note>original: *Ertzte*; an archaic spelling of *Ärzte*.</note> / and had them called to him /
but none had been able to help him /
so that he even commended his life to God /
and renounced all comfort of human help.

Then I began the **Anatomy of Herbs** /
I distilled them / and also extracted their **Salt** /
and the **quintessence** <term>fünffte Wesen</term> <note>Literally the "fifth essence" (Latin: *quinta essentia*). In alchemy, this is the purest, most concentrated medicinal force extracted from a substance, believed to be a celestial element beyond the four earthly elements.</note> / but I could find
none among them all / that could have
delivered the brother / even though I tried much /

<meta>catchword: for</meta>

<summary>Basilius Valentinus describes his transition from studying ancient monastic texts to performing practical alchemical experiments (distillation and extraction) in an attempt to cure a fellow monk suffering from kidney stones.</summary>
<keywords>monastic library, alchemical anatomy, kidney stones, physicians, distillation, salt, quintessence, Basilius Valentinus</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Preface" of Basilius Valentinus. The author, having found herbal medicine insufficient for his brother’s severe kidney stones, describes his transition from the study of plants (botany) to the study of minerals and metals (alchemy/chemistry).</meta>

# PREFACE.

for they were not of a high enough degree <note>original: *Grad*; in Galenic medicine, the "degree" refers to the intensity of a medicine's primary qualities—heat, cold, moisture, or dryness.</note> to help such an evil, and I could find no more herbs that I had not turned over and examined within six years.

Then I began to sharpen my thoughts and ponder the matter further; and I decided that I would also undertake to learn and to acquire a **science** <term>Wissenschafft</term> <note>In this context, "science" refers to a systematic, divinely-inspired knowledge of the natural world rather than modern secular science.</note> of the foundation which the **Creator** had placed into the **Metals** and **Minerals** of the earth. And the more I sought, the more I found, for one fountain always flowed out of another; and God granted me the fortune that I experienced much, and my eyes saw so many of the wondrous things which **Nature** had planted into the minerals and metals, that it is difficult for the ignorant to comprehend.

Among all these, I obtained a mineral which was composed of many colors and is also capable of much in the **Art** <note>original: *Kunst*; a common shorthand for the "Royal Art" of Alchemy.</note>. From it, I took its **spiritual essence** <note>original: *geistliches Wesen*; the volatile, active medicinal principle extracted from a raw mineral.</note> and with it made my sick brother healthy in a few days. For this spirit was very strong and also made the spirit of my brother strong; for he prayed his daily hour for me until he died; for he lived yet

<summary>Basilius Valentinus describes his transition from herbalism to mineral alchemy after six years of unsuccessful botanical research. He recount how he successfully cured his fellow monk using a "spiritual essence" extracted from a multi-colored mineral.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, mineral medicine, spiritual essence, medicinal degrees, nature's wonders, pharmaceutical history</keywords>

# PREFACE.

for a long time thereafter, once he had recovered; and his prayer and mine accomplished so much that the **Creator** <note>original: *Schöpffer*</note> revealed to me, and through my diligence allowed me to see, that which remains hidden even from the "clever" ones, as they call themselves.

And so, I have wished to indicate and—as much as has been permitted to me—reveal this concept, which gives you the **Stone of the Ancients** <note>original: *Stein der Vhralten*; a reference to the Philosopher's Stone, the legendary substance capable of curing all diseases and transmuting metals.</note> for the health and sustenance of mankind in this **valley of tears** <note>original: *Jammerthal*; a common historical metaphor for the suffering of earthly life compared to the peace of the afterlife.</note>. This stone was given to us from above as the highest earthly treasure. As you shall read, I have written this not for my own sake, but for the benefit of those who come after me. Having received guidance from the writings of many experienced men, I hope that you—through my report, which is devoted to **Philosophy** <term>Philosophey</term> <note>In this era, "Philosophy" refers to "Natural Philosophy," the study of nature and the physical universe, which included the science of alchemy.</note> and which is very brief but of vast substance—may find a rock upon which **Truth** <note>original: *Warheit*</note> can be built, and thereby attain a temporal reward, while wishing for the 

->attainment of the Eternal,<-

->AMEN.<-

# HERMANN KIRCHNER, DOCTOR OF BOTH LAWS <note>original: *J. U. D.*, shorthand for *Juris Utriusque Doctor*, referring to Civil and Canon law.</note>,
## Imperial Count Palatine, Professor at Marburg
->to Johannes Tholdius, Senator<-
->of Frankenhausen in Thuringia.<-

While others work the salt-pans by the hand of Vulcan <note>original: *Vulcano operante*; Vulcan is the Roman god of fire and smithing, here representing the intense heat required to evaporate brine into salt.</note>,
->And provide and prepare seasoning for food.<-
A useful task! For what is a table without salt? But you, noble Thöldius,
->Still perform a more useful work as an author.<-
For indeed, you spread the very salt of the mind and the wisdom of Plato,
->And your fragrance across every sea.<-
I am pleased to proclaim: more fortunate in life is he
->Who seeks, O Thöldius, to obtain your salt <note>Kirchner is making a sophisticated pun. Tholdius was an expert in salt-mining, but here "salt" refers to *Sal Sapientiae* (the Salt of Wisdom), a key alchemical principle and a metaphor for wit and intelligence.</note>.<-

# To Basilius Valentinus,
### most learned author of the Theophrastic discipline <term>Theophrasticæ disciplinæ</term> <note>This refers to the school of thought established by Paracelsus (Theophrastus von Hohenheim), which revolutionized medicine by using minerals and chemicals rather than just herbs.</note>,
->published by the most learned man, Johannes<-
->Tholdius.<-

The writings of Valentinus, hidden for many ages,
->Tholdius brings forth as a noble work into the light.<-
The author holds concealed riches, and the elements of things
->Which are situated far from the knowledge of the common crowd.<-
May Tholdius enjoy the praise for this discovered treasure,
->And may he bear the rewards and honor of this preserved book.<-

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page serves as the opening dedicatory poem or "Elegy" for a work by Basilius Valentinus, edited by Johann Thölde. It establishes the author's pedigree by linking him to the traditions of Paracelsus and ancient wisdom.</meta>

# ELEGY ON THE LITTLE BOOK
## of Basilius Valentinus, published through the labor and study
## of the most distinguished man, Master Johann Thölde,
## Chamberlain of the city of Frankenhausen, a Lord and
## friend most highly revered.

Whoever you are, diligent reader, who runs through this little field of a book
And wishes to know the author’s names,
Perceive what the truthful Muse, in no false song,
Displays to be seen in alternating meters <note>This refers to the poem's elegiac couplet structure, which alternates between hexameter and pentameter lines</note>.
Basilius <note>Basilius Valentinus, the legendary but likely pseudonymous alchemist and monk</note>, when the Golden Age of old was in its prime,
Was endowed with a wondrous art.
And (lest the deceitful names of blind night cast anything before you)
His name was an omen of strength <note>original: "roboris" — This is a scholarly pun on the name "Valentinus," which is derived from the Latin *valere*, meaning to be strong or healthy</note>.
Do you ask what his study was? What path of life
He followed? He duly pressed upon a sacred journey.
Not like the tonsured crowds <note>A common Renaissance-era critique of monks and clergy, suggesting they were often idle or unlearned</note> of our own time,
Following leisure and the sin of sloth;
But by pondering sacred oracles in silent books,
He amplified the broad riches of his intellect.
Just as Zoroaster <note>The ancient Persian prophet, whom alchemists and Hermeticists regarded as a master of hidden wisdom; legend says he was the only child to laugh at his own birth</note>, who first came into the breezes with a laugh,
And was skilled in the hidden work of the art;
And just as Paracelsus <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), the famous Swiss physician and alchemist who pioneered the use of chemicals and minerals in medicine</note>, a strong refuge for the sick,
Whom I rightly consider to be a miracle of the world.
He wove divinely inspired medicines with physical cures,
So that he might be a physician of the body as well as the soul.
He unearthed mysteries for those weary with the study of metals,
And whatever secrets the seeds of flowers contain.

<meta>catchword: Whatever...</meta>

<summary>A dedicatory poem introducing the alchemist Basilius Valentinus, comparing his medical and alchemical wisdom to that of Zoroaster and Paracelsus. It emphasizes that his work serves both the body and the soul.</summary>
<keywords>Basilius Valentinus, Johann Thölde, alchemy, Paracelsus, Zoroaster, medicine, Frankenhausen, elegiac meter</keywords>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedicatory elegy for Basilius Valentinus's work, shifting focus from the legendary author to the contemporary editor, Johann Thölde. It establishes Thölde’s noble Hessian lineage and his professional life in the salt-producing town of Frankenhausen.</meta>

Whatever other animals bring forth from their hidden fibers <note>original: "fibris" — referring to the internal organs or "entrails" of animals, often used in early medicine to derive remedies</note>
To provide aid for injured limbs;
And how the Spirit, joined to the structure of the Soul, might labor
To provide for all kinds of natures <note>original: "physes" — from the Greek *physis*, meaning nature or the natural world</note> with a powerful sword;
So that nothing is missing here, provided you excel in mental sharpness
And drink in what the read page demonstrates.
The page of Johann, which the industry of Thölde produced,
Will now allow you to know these laws through clear signs.
Do you wonder from where he draws his lineage? Most famous 
Hesse <note>original: "Hassia" — a historical region in Germany</note> saw him take his first nourishment from a patrician mother.
And if you count back through many five-year spans <note>original: "lustra" — a Roman unit of time consisting of five years</note>, a crowd of his ancestors
Gave their faithful service to the Landgraves <note>The hereditary noble rulers of the German state of Hesse</note>,
Whether the white lilies of harmonious peace stood firm,
Or the fierce sword returned the fields to hostility.
Now he dwells with me in a place rich in ancient saltworks <note>original: "salinis" — referring to the town of Frankenhausen, famous for its salt springs, where Thölde worked</note>,
In which I form and reform an unformed flock <note>This likely refers to the author’s role as a teacher or official shaping the community</note>.
Perhaps he is worthy of a better Sparta <note>A classical idiom: "You have obtained Sparta; adorn it," meaning to make the best of one’s current lot in life, though the author suggests Thölde deserves even greater things</note>, if only
Fortune were not an enemy to me through wicked War.
As one part of the senate <note>The city council</note>, he rules the common people with reins,
And gathers many joys from "false pepper" <note>original: "falsi piperis" — This may be a cryptic or local reference to the salt trade or perhaps a metaphorical critique of deceptive commerce</note>.
He also turns secret volumes with attentive hands,
Which Philosophy holds concerning the secrets of nature.
To him I now offer unanimous thanks in a common name,
Because he has not suppressed this work in the slightest.
Just like a dog who holds a great morsel in its throat
By force—a piece that could be enough for many—
An envious man stirs up insane riots with a rabid mouth,
Lest any companion should enjoy the hard-won prey.
But this man, being kind, has given a fountain for generous use,
From which the reader may quench the damage of his thirst.

<summary>This page concludes the introductory poem, praising Johann Thölde for his noble ancestry in Hesse and his service as a councilman in the salt-mining town of Frankenhausen. It portrays Thölde as a generous scholar-official who, unlike "envious dogs" of knowledge, chooses to share these alchemical secrets with the public.</summary>
<keywords>Johann Thölde, Hesse, Frankenhausen, saltworks, Landgraves, alchemy, natural philosophy, patronage, salt trade</keywords>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedicatory sequence praising Johann Thölde. The previous page discussed Thölde’s noble Hessian lineage and his work in the salt-producing town of Frankenhausen. This page concludes the first poem with a plea for fair judgment and begins a second poem by Antonius Valdenberger, emphasizing Thölde’s role in bringing the "Monk’s" (Basilius Valentinus) alchemical secrets to light.</meta>

Just as bees and just as oxen, who fashion honeyed
Gifts, and who draw heavy plows with their backs;
They labor not for themselves but for us, so that the soft weariness
Of life might be eased: so that for many, there might be many good things amidst the bad.
Yet you, young man, before you give your censure
Of the poet's substantial decrees, use your nose <note>original: "utere nare" — a classical idiom for using one’s sense of smell or discernment to detect quality or sarcasm</note> first.
And first, search through the whole little book, turned over thoroughly
(Judgment usually stands firm once the matter is well read);
I do not doubt that you will give these words your highest praise:
"May it be well, may it be well with Thölde for a thousand ages."

->*By*<-
->***Antonius V. Valdenberger,***<-
->***A Hessian from Hersfeld.***<-

### TO JOHANN THÖLDE,
### Senator of Frankenhausen, his
### dearest friend.

Should I not rightly congratulate you, John <note>original: "Jane" — the vocative form of Joannes or Johannes</note>, because you handle
Alchemical matters <note>original: "Chymica" — referring to the art of alchemy and early chemistry</note>, and desire to be useful to all?
Because you handle alchemy in such a way that anyone can recognize
What profit they may take from it for themselves.
Nor did you regret having pored over the books of the MONK <note>original: "MONACHI" — referring to Basilius Valentinus, the legendary Benedictine monk whose alchemical writings Thölde edited and published</note>,
From which you gather this goodness with wondrous skill.
And like a Bee, you bring forth chosen treasures in order,
Teaching with clear words and reason.
You do not act like those who now, idle with the title of "Doctor,"
Pretend to be Bucephaluses <note>The famous, spirited horse of Alexander the Great; here used to represent a noble or powerful intellectual front</note> while concealing the fact that they are asses.

<summary>This page concludes a poetic tribute to Johann Thölde, comparing his hard work to that of bees and oxen, and begins a new dedication by Antonius Valdenberger praising Thölde's skill in editing the alchemical works of "the Monk" (Basilius Valentinus).</summary>
<keywords>Johann Thölde, Antonius Valdenberger, alchemy, Basilius Valentinus, Frankenhausen, scientific editing, Bucephalus metaphor</keywords>

--- Page 25 ---
<meta>This page continues a series of commendatory or vituperative verses regarding the proper study of ancient texts versus the abuses of pseudo-alchemy. The previous page likely introduced the recipient of this poem, praising their scholarly integrity.</meta>

Nor do you act as that bold, blasphemous man of Leipzig <note>The "Lipsicus" mentioned here is likely a contemporary figure from Leipzig whom the author accuses of misinterpreting scripture for alchemical purposes</note> did,
Who makes the mystical rites of the **TRINITY** <note>original: TRIADOS</note> profane.
He falsely claims that the articles of the holy faith, the sacred Creeds, and the Psalms
Properly teach of his "Stone" <note>Referring to the Philosopher's Stone, the legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold</note>.
This Stone which the "Philosophers" <note>A common term for alchemists during this period</note> have imagined for themselves,
Which would immediately produce the gold of **MIDAS**.
He claims it is the eternal <term>Word</term> <note>original: logos (λογος); a central theological concept representing the second person of the Trinity</note>, or that Holy Spirit,
Or the Father, or this **NOTHINGNESS** which existed before
->Chaos.<-
If anyone should possess this stone, he will generate mountains of gold,
Becoming wealthier and better than ancient kings.
Beggars in the meantime, they have had no food,
Except what a lying tongue produced through deceits.
May the commonwealth of Christ drive this breed out from Christ's assembly;
May the divine vengeance of God drive them from the nations.
As this breed sells smoke <note>A common Latin idiom, "fumos vendere," meaning to sell empty promises or to deceive people with vaporous lies</note>, so may a smoky flame
Consume them <note>The author uses "sublimet," an alchemical term for turning a solid to vapor, here punning on the execution of heretics or frauds</note>, or the new gallows of the Duke of Swabia.
But may you continue to bring forth the papers of the learned ancients
And reveal unknown writings to good men.
Thus, for your pleasing deed, grace shall be born for you:
Thus you shall be the glory of a grateful posterity.

->**Albertus Hageman,**<-
->*of Uelzen.*<-

Just as the bees and just as the oxen, who produce honeyed
Gifts, and who pull the strong plows with their backs.
Not for themselves but for us they labor, so that the weariness of a soft
Life is eased: so that there may be many good things for many in times of trouble.
Yet you, young man, do not dismiss these poet's words as vain
Until you have first used your nose <note>original: nare; a common Latin metaphor for discernment or a "scent" for the truth</note> to judge them.
And first examine the whole little book inside and out
(Judgment usually stands firm when a thing has been read well;)

->By<-

# TO JOHANN THOLDE,
## Senator of Frankenhausen, his
### dearest friend.

Does it not rightly, John <note>original: Jane; a Latinized form of Johann/John</note>, behoove me to congratulate you, because you handle
->alchemy?<- <note>original: Chymica; referring to the study of chemistry and alchemy, which were not yet distinct sciences</note>
And you desire to be useful to all?
Because you handle alchemy in such a way that anyone can know
What profit they may take from it for themselves.
Nor did it repent you to have wanted the books of the MONK <note>This refers to Basilius Valentinus, a legendary 15th-century Benedictine monk whose alchemical writings were famously edited and published by Johann Tholde</note>,
From which you gather this good knowledge with wonderful art.
And like a Bee, you bring forth the chosen treasures in order,
You teach with clear words and with reason.
You do not act like those who, now idle with the title of Doctor,
Pretend to be Bucephali <note>The noble war-horse of Alexander the Great; used here to represent someone pretending to be a great scholar</note> but hide that they are asses.

<summary>This page contains two poems: the conclusion of a piece by Anton Waldenberger praising diligent labor, and the beginning of a poem to Johann Tholde praising his clear and honest presentation of the alchemical works of "the Monk" (Basilius Valentinus).</summary>

--- Page 27 ---
<meta>This page continues the series of commendatory poems addressed to Johann Thölde. While the previous page ended with an analogy comparing Thölde to a diligent bee, this page shifts to a sharp critique of fraudulent alchemists—specifically a "blasphemer from Leipzig"—contrasting their deceptive claims with Thölde’s honest editorial work in recovering ancient texts.</meta>

Nor do you act as the bold, blasphemous man from Leipzig <note>original: Lipsicus; likely a reference to a specific contemporary critic or a rival alchemist from the city of Leipzig</note> did,
Who turns the mystical rites of the <term>TRINITY</term> <note>original: Triados</note> into something profane.
He falsely claims that the articles of the sacred faith, the holy Creeds, and the Psalms
Properly teach of his "Stone" <note>referring to the Philosopher's Stone, the legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold</note>.
This Stone which the "Philosophers" <note>Alchemists often referred to themselves as "natural philosophers"</note> have fashioned for themselves,
Which would immediately produce the gold of <term>MIDAS</term> <note>The mythical king whose touch turned everything to gold</note>.
[He claims] that it is the eternal <term>WORD</term> <note>original: logos; in Christian theology, this refers to Christ as the divine reason</note>, or that Holy Spirit,
Or the Father, or <term>THIS NOTHING</term> <note>original: nihil hoc</note>, which existed before Chaos.
If anyone should possess this, he will generate mountains of gold,
Richer and better than the kings of old.
Meanwhile, they are beggars, who have had no food
Except what their lying tongues have produced through deceits.
May the Commonwealth of Christ drive this breed from the assembly of Christ,
And the divine vengeance of God drive them from the people.
As this breed sells smoke <note>a common historical metaphor for selling empty promises or fraudulent schemes</note>, so may a smoky flame
Exalt them <note>original: sublimet; a clever pun on the alchemical process of "sublimation" (turning a solid into vapor) and being "raised up" on a gallows</note>, or the new gallows of the Duke of Swabia <note>original: Sueviae</note>.
May you continue to bring forth the papers of the learned men of old,
And reveal unknown writings to good men.
Thus, for your pleasing deed, favor shall be born for you;
Thus you will be the glory of a grateful posterity.

->**Albert Hageman**,<-
->*from Uelzen.*<-

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the previous page's condemnation of "smoke-selling" fraudulent alchemists to a direct commendation of Johann Thölde, the editor. It introduces the legendary author Basilius Valentinus and describes the book’s contents as a study of nature’s secrets across the animal, vegetable, and mineral kingdoms.</meta>

## That this book was brought to light and published for the common good at the insistence and request of many distinguished people by the most honorable, respected, and very wise Mr. Johann Thölde, Chamberlain <note>original: Camerario; an administrative official in charge of finances</note> of the Council and Master of the Salt-Pans <note>original: Pfannenherrn; a high-ranking supervisor of the salt evaporation works</note> of the praiseworthy Saltworks at Frankenhausen; I, the undersigned, have prepared and writ-
->ten these verses in his<-
->praise and special honor.<-

This book, which is prepared for you,
With art and many secrets,
To be explored through God's gift,
Which He has bestowed upon Nature,
His creation high, entirely wise without end,
Upon the Earth and the firmament,
How all things are able to grow,
And spring forth from the elements,
How they are nourished and sustained,
And can be prepared here on Earth,
Into their effective virtuous power,
To obtain true strength,
From animals, herbs, and metals,
And from all manner of many wondrous things,
[This book] was written by one named Basilius Valentinus <note>A legendary 15th-century alchemist and Benedictine monk; most modern scholars believe this was actually a pseudonym used by the editor, Johann Thölde</note>,
A clergyman by his station,

<summary>This page serves as a formal dedication to the editor Johann Thölde, praising his role in publishing the alchemical secrets of Basilius Valentinus. The introductory poem frames alchemy as a divine gift for understanding the growth and hidden powers of all natural things.</summary>

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory section from page 22. The previous page ended by identifying Basilius Valentinus as a clergyman; this page concludes the commendatory poem by Christoph Bierbach, shifting focus to the editor Johann Thölde and his efforts in publishing the work.</meta>

Though he departed many years ago <note>original: "verlassen"; likely referring to the death of the supposed author, Basilius Valentinus</note>,
Great praise justly remains for his teaching <note>original: "Lahr" (Lehre); his doctrine or instruction</note>,
As a physician sent by God,
Who recognized the foundation of all things.
Offer praise now for such a work <note>original: "Schrifft"; the manuscript or text being presented</note>,
Which Basilius has prepared.
To Johann Thölde from the land of Hesse,
Now well-known in Frankenhausen <note>A town in Thuringia, Germany, significant for its salt production where Thölde served as an official</note>,
Who through his toil and diligent labor,
Has brought the same into print;
He also regretted no effort at any hour,
Seeking out the foundation of all things.
Therefore thank him for this book;
Now seek further instruction from him.
May God also grant him for this:
Blessing in this time, and eternal life hereafter.

->Christoph Bierbach<-

He spared no effort at any hour
To research all things from their very foundation.

Seek further instruction from him now.
May God also grant him in return
Blessings here in this life, and eternal life hereafter.

# Concerning the Great Stone of the Ancient Sages, by Brother Basil Valentine of the Benedictine Order.

In my preface, dear friend and devotee of <term>the Art<note>The "Art" refers to alchemy, specifically the "Great Work" (Magnum Opus) of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</note></term>, I gave an assurance to you and others who are so eager to learn the properties of Nature and to research the arts from the heart, that I would point out the cornerstone and the rock—as far as has been permitted to me and granted from above—from which our ancestors and the ancients prepared their Stone. This Stone, which they received from the Most High, was prepared so that they might use it for their health and the sustenance of this earthly life. So that I may now fulfill my promise and pledge, and not lead you into many detours through the errors of <term>sophistry<note>In this context, "sophistry" refers to the deceptive or misguided practices of "puffers"—fraudulent alchemists who lacked spiritual insight and focused only on literal, material chemical recipes.</note></term>, I shall instead reveal to you the main source of all goodness from its very foundation.

Therefore, you should know, and take good heed of my following discourse, if you otherwise...

<summary>In this opening section, Basil Valentine promises to reveal the true foundation of the Philosopher's Stone, which was used by ancient sages for health and long life, while warning the reader against the false paths of alchemical charlatans.</summary>
<keywords>Basil Valentine, Philosopher's Stone, alchemy, Benedictine, sophistry, health, ancient sages</keywords>

# Concerning the Great Stone

...carry an inclination to learn <term>the Art<note>The "Art" refers to alchemy, specifically the "Great Work" (Magnum Opus).</note></term>; for I cannot provide much empty chatter, nor is it my intention, since there is very little to be learned from it; instead, I prefer brief words that possess a solid foundation.

Now you should know that you will come little toward attaining this mastery, even if you build ever so much upon our <term>Stone<note>The Philosopher's Stone, the legendary substance capable of transmuting base metals into gold and providing an elixir of life.</note></term>, for the Creator has not granted true knowledge and its attainment to everyone. Instead, He has wished to bestow it upon those who hate lies and allow for the truth, who also devote themselves to the Art with sighs from the heart and pursue it; but purely to those who love Him without any falseness and pray for it.

Therefore, I tell you in pure truth: if you wish to make our great, ancient Stone, then follow my teaching and pray above all things to your God, the Creator of all creatures, that He might grant you blessing and prosperity for it. If you have also sinned, then confess and do good, and keep in your intention that you will no longer be evil, but wish to be pious, and that your heart may be enlightened in all good things; and remember, when you are placed in honor, that

### of the Ancients.

...[if] you offer [charity] to the poor and needy, to rescue them from misery and gladden them with your helpful hand, so that you may receive all the more blessing from the Lord and obtain a seat in heaven through the confirmation of your faith.

My friend, do not despise nor disdain to read the truthful writings of those who possessed the **Stone** before us. For through God’s revelation, I have received this knowledge from them. Such reading must be done frequently and repeatedly, so that the foundation is not forgotten and the truth does not go out like a flickering lamp.

Furthermore, do not forget your diligent labor, searching the scriptures steadily. Do not let your purpose be shaken by <term>fickleness<note>The original German *Wanckelmuht* refers to an inconstant or wavering mind. For a fickle person often takes the wrong path and loses his way on the road; likewise, people of a wavering mind rarely build houses that endure.

# Of the Great Stone

For this **Stone** is secure against all danger of **fire**; therefore, you may well refrain from seeking it in those things where Nature does not permit it to be found.

It is as if one were to say it were a <term>vegetable work<note>German: *Vegetabelwerck*. In early chemistry and alchemy, materials were classified into three "kingdoms": Mineral, Vegetable, and Animal. The author is arguing that while the Stone shares characteristics with plants, it is not a plant itself.</note></term>, which yet cannot be, even though it possesses a certain growth. For take note: if it were with our Stone as it is with any other herb, it would easily burn away so that nothing remained but a mere <term>salt<note>German: *Salz*. Alchemists believed that burning a plant destroyed its "sulfur" (oil/soul) and "mercury" (spirit), leaving behind only the "salt" (body/ash).</note></term>. And although those who came before me have written much concerning the **Vegetable Stone**, you should know, my friend, that this will be very difficult for you to grasp; for because our Stone grows and increases itself, they have called it a **Vegetable Stone**.

You should further know that irrational **animals** cannot bring about any increase of their kind unless it happens according to the nature of their own kind. Therefore, you must not seek the true Stone, nor undertake to make it, except from its own **seed**, from which our Stone itself was made from the very beginning. From this, conclude and understand, my

### Of the Great Stone

For this **Stone** is secure against all danger of **fire**; therefore, you may well refrain from seeking it in those things where **Nature** has not permitted it to be found.

You should further know that irrational animals cannot bring about any multiplication of their kind except from a nature like their own; therefore, you must not seek the **true Stone**, nor undertake to make it, from anything except its own **seed**, from which our **Stone** itself has been made from the very beginning. # ->of the Ancients.<-

Friend, you should also take care that you do not choose to seek any **animal soul** <note>Original: *animalische Seele*. The author is warning against using biological materials like blood, hair, or flesh in the alchemical process.</note> for this purpose. For **flesh and blood**, just as they were granted and given to the beasts by the **Creator**, belong only to the beasts; they are the materials from which God built them, so that a living creature resulted. Instead, our **Stone**, which I have inherited from the **Ancients**, comes from and is made of **two things and yet one thing**, which keep a **third** hidden.

<margin>Sulphur</margin> This is the pure truth, and rightly spoken; <margin>Mercury</margin> for "man" and "woman" were understood by the **Ancients** to be a single body—not in their outward appearance, but because of their implanted love and the natural internal workings they have possessed from the beginning. Just as these two are recognized as one and are able to propagate and multiply their **seed**, so too can the **seed** of the **matter** from which our **Stone** is made be propagated and multiplied.

If you are now a true lover of our **Art** <note>A common historical term for the practice of Alchemy.</note>, you will hold these words in high esteem and reflect upon them wisely, so that you do not fall into the pit dug by the enemy, alongside other blind <term>sophists<note>In this context, "sophists" refers to "puffers" or false alchemists who lacked true philosophical understanding and misled others with deceptive or incorrect processes.</note></term>.

# ->Of the Great Stone<-

But so that you may know, my friend, where such **seed** originates, ask yourself: For what purpose do you desire to seek our **Stone**? Thus it will become manifest to you that it must flow from nowhere else than a **metallic root** <note>The author argues that the "seed" of the Philosopher’s Stone must share the nature of the metals it is intended to transform.</note>, from which the **Creator** also ordained the metals themselves to be born. How this happens, mark well:

In the beginning, when the Spirit hovered over the waters and all was surrounded by darkness, the Almighty and eternal God—whose beginning has no end, and whose wisdom has been from the start and stems from eternity—created from His inscrutable counsel Heaven and Earth, and everything contained therein, visible and invisible, by whatever name it may be called, out of nothing. For God made all things from nothing <note>original: "Omnia enim Deus fecit ex nihilo." This refers to the theological concept of *Creatio ex nihilo*, suggesting that the divine power to create matter is the ultimate foundation of alchemical work.</note>. As to how such a glorious creation came to pass, I do not wish to be a **philosopher** <note>Here, "philosopher" refers to a natural philosopher or scientist attempting to explain the physical mechanics of the universe.</note> regarding that now, for Scripture and faith must judge it.

In such a creation, the **Creator** endowed every nature so that it might not fall into decay and waste—

### der Vhralten. <note>original: "of the Ancients"</note>

—might be given their **seed**, so that through it a **multiplication** might occur, and humans and animals, herbs and metal would be preserved. It is not permitted to man, and is against the **Order of God**, to make a new seed according to his own liking; rather, only a propagation and multiplication is allowed. For the making of the seed, the **highest Creator** has reserved for Himself alone; otherwise, it would be possible for man also to become a creator, which cannot be, but belongs solely to the glory of the **Most High**.

Now know regarding this **seed**, which works the metals, thus: that a **heavenly influx** <note>The author refers to *influentia*, the belief that spiritual or celestial forces "flow" down into material substances to give them life or specific properties.</note> falls down from above through God’s permission and order, and mixes itself with the **sidereal properties** <term>sidereal: relating to the stars and planets, which were believed to govern the growth and characteristics of metals underground</term>. This is the beginning of our **seed**, its first origin, and that by which it can prove the ancestry of its birth. # Of the Great Stone

—the Etnean fire <note>The author uses "Etnean fire" (referring to the volcano Mount Etna) as a metaphor for the constant, intense natural heat found within the earth or the alchemist's furnace.</note> work on and on, until they bring about a perfect thing, which Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Greek-Egyptian figure credited with founding the "Hermetic" art of alchemy.</note> and all who came before me—since we can find nothing else—have called the Three First Things from the beginning of the Mastery; and there has been found an internal soul, an incomprehensible spirit, and a corporeal, visible appearance. ### of the Ancients.

it is certain that a metallic **Mercury**, a metallic **Sulfur** <note>original: "Schwebel." An archaic German term for Schwefel (sulfur).</note>, and a metallic **Salt** must follow; they can, of necessity, yield nothing else but a perfect **Metallic Body** <term>Corpus: Latin for "body"; in alchemical terms, the physical, tangible manifestation of a substance.</term>.

If you will not now understand what you ought to understand, you will not be devoted to **Philosophy** <note>In this context, "Philosophy" refers to the "Hermetic Philosophy" or the Great Art of Alchemy.</note>, or God will not grant it to you.

And I say briefly, that it will not be possible for you to attain the blessing of utility in metallic form, unless you have brought the three aforementioned beginnings together into one without defect. For understand further, that the animals of the earth are composed of flesh and blood, just as man is; they also have a living spirit and breath that dwells within them and possesses them, just like man; but they lack a **rational soul** <note>The author distinguishes between the "vital spirit" (animating breath) and the "rational soul" (the eternal, divine intellect), a common theological and philosophical distinction of the era.</note>, with which man is gifted above other animals. Therefore, when the irrational <note>original: "vernünfftigen." This is likely a printer's error in the original text; the context clearly refers to the "un-rational" animals who lack the soul mentioned in the previous sentence.</note> animals lose their life through death and pass away, it is completely over with them, and there is nothing more to be hoped for from them at any time.

# Of the Great Stone

But man, who must sacrifice his life to his **Creator** <note>original: "Schöpffer."</note> through temporal death, nevertheless lives in his soul; and when he is **clarified** <term>clarificirt: From the Latin *clarificare*, meaning to make clear or bright. In alchemy, this refers to the purification of a substance; in a theological context, it refers to the "glorification" of the body in the afterlife.</term>, the soul will once again make its dwelling in the **glorified body** <note>original: "verklärten Leibe."</note>. Thus **Body, Soul, and Spirit** come together again and will then manifest their heavenly **glorification** <note>original: "Erklerung." In this context, it refers to a transformation into a state of light or divine clarity.</note>, which will never again allow themselves to be separated for all eternity, etc.

Therefore, man is to be recognized as a **fixed creature** <term>fixe Creatur: In alchemy, a "fixed" substance is one that is stable and does not evaporate or change when exposed to heat. The author uses this to describe the soul's immortality.</term> because of his soul, for (even though he must die according to outward appearance) he remains in eternity after this life. For the death of man is only a **glorification**, in that he—through certain **degrees** <note>original: "gradus." This implies a step-by-step process of refinement, much like an alchemical experiment.</note> ordained by God—is redeemed from the sinful and placed into a better state. This, however, does not happen to the animals; therefore, they are to be recognized as **un-fixed creatures**, for after their passing they have no further **resuscitation** <note>original: "Resuscitation." Used here to mean resurrection or being brought back to life.</note> to expect, because they lack the **rational soul** for which the only true **Mediator** <note>original: "Mittler." A traditional title for Jesus Christ.</note>, the Son of God, suffered and shed His blood. A spirit can indeed

...dwell in one body; yet it does not follow from this that it would be constant <note>original: "bestendig." # Of the Great Stone

...and to overcome all enemies, which is found in **Sol** <note>original: "Sole." Latin for the Sun, the alchemical symbol for gold, considered the only perfect metal.</note> alone. **Luna** <note>original: "Luna." Latin for the Moon, representing silver.</note> has a fixed **Mercury** <note>original: "Mercurium."</note> with her; therefore, she does not fly away in the **Fire** so quickly as the other imperfect metals, but instead withstands her examination in the fire, and proves this clearly through victory and triumph, because the devouring **Saturn** <note>Saturn: The alchemical name for lead. In the process of cupellation, lead is used to "devour" or absorb impurities, but it cannot consume silver or gold.</note> can hunt down no prey from her.

The arch-mistress **Venus** <note>original: "Erzbulerin." This personification refers to copper, which was often associated with beauty and vanity, but also corruption.</note> is clothed and occupied with an abundance of color, and her masterly body is pure **Tincture** <term>Tincture: In alchemy, a concentrated spiritual essence or "color" that has the power to transform and "tinge" the nature of other substances.</term>, and is exactly like that color which also dwells in the best metal, and because of its abundance, it manifests as red. And because her body is leprous <note>original: "aussetzig." Alchemists used the term "leprosy" to describe the corrosion, oxidation, or perceived "sickness" and impurity found in base metals like copper.</note>, the constant **Tincture** can have no permanent dwelling in that inconstant body, but must vanish together with its body; for where the body is consumed through killing <note>original: "Tödtung." The alchemical process of "mortification," where a substance's outward form is destroyed to release its inner spirit.</note>, the body cannot remain either, but must give way and flee, for the dwelling has been destroyed and burnt with fire. Thus, its place is no longer to be recognized, nor can anyone dwell there any further; however, in a fixed <note>original: "figirten." Likely a variant of "fixiert," meaning a substance that has been made resistant to fire and evaporation.</note> body, it gladly dwells with constancy.

# Of the Great Stone

...justify the **Sulfur** and the **Salt** (meaning those of the Sages) <note>Sulfur and Salt, along with Mercury, are the "Three Primes" (Tria Prima), the fundamental components of all matter in alchemical theory.</note> in such a way that from that same **Soul**, its **Spirit**, and its **Body** <note>In alchemy, these correspond to the Three Primes: Sulfur (Soul), Mercury (Spirit), and Salt (Body).</note> a completely inseparable union occurs, which does not allow itself to be separated for all eternity, nor can it ever again be parted from one another; then the **bond of love** is perfectly prepared, and the **dwelling of the crown** <note>A poetic reference to the completed Philosopher's Stone, often called the "Crown of Victory."</note> is sufficiently readied. 

And know that this is nothing else but a **liquid key**, to be compared to heavenly properties, and a **dry water** <note>original: "trockenes Wasser." This is a classic alchemical paradox referring to a substance that acts like a liquid solvent but is composed of metallic or "dry" earth—often identified with "Philosophical Mercury."</note> joined to an earthly substance, all of which is a single thing, having come and grown from **three, two, and one** <note>This likely refers to the Three Primes, the two principles of Male/Female (Sulfur/Mercury), and the final One (the Stone).</note>. If you can achieve this, then you have already won the mastery, *and then marry the **Bride** and **Bridegroom** to one another, so that they may feed and nourish themselves well with their own flesh and blood, and multiply infinitely through their own seed.* <note>The marriage of the Bride and Bridegroom is a central alchemical allegory for the chemical wedding, or the union of the fixed and volatile principles.</note> 

And though I would gladly reveal more to you out of love, the **Creator** has forbidden me to do so; therefore, it is not fitting for me to speak further of this, so that the gift of the **Most High** is not abused, and I may not be the cause of committing a grave sin.

him; and made a very sharp speech, to this effect:

> I, **Saturn** <term>Saturnus: In alchemy, Saturn represents the metal Lead and the stage of *Nigredo* (blackness or putrefaction), the necessary first step of decay in the Great Work.</term>, the highest planet in the **firmament** <term>Firmament: The celestial sphere of the stars, which in ancient cosmology was believed to surround the Earth.</term>, hereby testify before all you lords, that I am the most incapable and least-esteemed among you all, possessed of a weak, destructible body, of **black color**, subject to much fragility in this **vale of tears** <note>original: "Jammerthal." A common religious and poetic term for the mortal world, here applied to the base, "fallen" state of unrefined matter.</note>, and yet a tester of you all; for I have no permanent city, and I take with me those like myself; none are to blame for this my misery but the inconstant **Mercury** <term>Mercurio: The alchemical name for Quicksilver, often characterized as a "flighty" or "unstable" spirit that must be "fixed" or stabilized.</term>, who through his neglect and sloth has inflicted such evil upon me; therefore I ask you lords together to avenge this upon him, and since he has already been thrown into prison, to kill him completely, and to let him rot therein entirely, until one can no longer recognize a single drop of his **blood** <note>In alchemy, the "blood" of a metal often refers to its essential spirit or the red color it takes on during specific chemical reactions.</note>.

As Saturn had now concluded his speech, the gray **Jupiter** <term>Jupiter: The alchemical symbol for the metal Tin; "gray" refers to the dull, metallic appearance of the element.</term> stepped forward, and made a short introduction to his speech with bent knees and a special reverence of his **scepter** <note>The scepter symbolizes the ruling power of the planet/metal over a specific stage of the chemical process.</note>, thereupon praising the proposal of his companion Saturn, and commanded

# Of the Great Stone

->42<-

...to persecute all those who did not help to further this **execution**; and with this, he made an end.

After this came **Mars** <term>Mars: In alchemical symbolism, Mars represents the metal Iron and is often associated with the color red and the forceful energy required to "break" the chemical bonds of base matter.</term> with a naked **sword**; it was covered in wonderful colors, just like pure fiery mirrors, which cast out strange and adventurous rays. He gave the sword to the jailer **Vulcan** <term>Vulcanus: The Roman god of the forge, representing the alchemist’s fire and the intense heat of the furnace used to "punish" or refine the substances.</term>, so that he might carry out all that had been decreed by the Lord; and when he had killed **Mercury** <term>Mercurium: Quicksilver. In this, **Vulcan**, acting as an **executioner** <note>original: "Nachrichter." An old term for an executioner, literally one who "judges after" or carries out the sentence.</note>, was willing to obey.

Now when the executioner had performed his office, a beautiful, white-shining woman stepped forward in long silver-colored clothes, interwoven with many waters. When she was properly recognized, it was perceived that she was **Luna** <term>Luna: The Moon, representing the metal Silver and the cool, feminine, "volatile" principle in the Great Work.</term>, the wife of **Sol** <note>original: "Solis Haußfrau." In Latin, Solis means "of the Sun." In alchemy, the "Marriage of the Sun and Moon" represents the union of opposites (Gold and Silver, Fixed and Volatile).</note>. She fell down upon her face and pleaded fervently with sighs and the shedding of tears for her husband, the **Sun**, to be released from the prison into which **Mercury** had brought him by cunning and force in a deceitful manner; and now, by the command of the other planets, both...

### Of the Great Stone

<meta>catchword: and</meta>

...granted by the gods, to lead them to the great **Wisdom**; behold, O Human, look upon it right well and follow its radiance, for within them Wisdom is found.

The swift **bird from the South** <note>original: "Vogel vom Mittage." In alchemy, the South (Midday) is often associated with the element of Fire and the peak of the work's intensity. Give the beast from the Rising Sun wings also, like the bird from the South, so that they may become equal; for the beast of the Rising Sun must be stripped of its **lion skin** <term>Lion Skin: Refers to the "Green Lion" or the outer, impure form of the metal that must be removed to reach the "gold" within.</term>, and its wings must disappear again, for both must enter into the **great salty sea** <note>The "salty sea" refers to the *Mercurial* solvent or the "Primordial Water" in which the substances are dissolved to be purified.</note> and come out again with beauty. **Hungary** <note>original: "Vngerland." Hungary was famous in the Renaissance for its rich mineral mines, particularly "Hungarian Vitriol," which alchemists believed was a primary ingredient for the Stone.</note> first gave me birth; the heaven and the stars maintain me, and the earth suckles me. ## Of the Great Stone

[be] very afraid / that the earthiness <note>original: *Erdreich*. In alchemy, "earth" represents the solid, non-volatile part of a substance that must be purified.</note> must vanish / and fly through all the air / and also rise into the highest heights of the uppermost heaven / then burn off its wings <note>The "burning of wings" is a common alchemical metaphor for "fixation"—the process of making a volatile substance stable so it no longer evaporates when heated.</note> with the very greatest fire / so that the earthiness must fall and plunge into the Red Sea <note>The "Red Sea" often symbolizes the final stage of the alchemical work (the *Rubedo* or Redness) or a specific mercurial solvent.</note> / and drown therein / Then command the sea to stand still / and dry up the water through fire and air / so that it becomes earth once again / Thus you have / I say / **ALL in ALL** / If you cannot find such / then reach into your own bosom / and around you into all things / that are found in the whole world / then you will find **ALL in ALL** / which is an attracting power / coming from all metallic and mineral things out of Salt and Sulfur <note>original: "ex Sale vnd Sulphure." Along with Mercury, these are the *Tria Prima* (Three Primaries) that alchemists believed composed all material bodies.</note> / and born twice from Mercury <note>original: "Mercurio." Mercury was considered the "mother" of all metals; being "born twice" likely refers to the chemical processes of distillation and sublimation.</note> / More (I say) it is not proper for me to say / of All in All / because All is contained in All.

&emsp;&emsp;When this speech was finished / he spoke further / You dear men / thus you will have received wisdom through the hearing of my voice / from whence and how you [shall] find <note>The original text ends with the partial word "erfin-", the start of *erfinden* (to find or discover).</note> the great stone of the ancient, prudent masters—

# Of the Great Stone

My friend should know that all impure and defiled things are unworthy <note>original: "indigni"</note> of our work, for their leprosy <note>In alchemical texts, "leprosy" refers to the corrosion, oxidation, or impurities found in base metals that must be removed.</note> can yield no advancement to our work, and the good is hindered by the impure path.

All merchant's goods from the mountains are worth their price; but if adulteration is introduced, the goods become unfit, for they are falsified and are no longer as they were before in their operation <note>Refers to the functional or chemical effectiveness of a substance.</note>.

And just as the physician sweeps out and cleanses the internal body by means of his medicine, and drives out all impurity from it, so must our bodies <note>In this context, "bodies" refers to the metallic or chemical substances being worked upon in the laboratory.</note> also be swept and purged <note>original: "purgirt"</note> of all impurity, so that perfection may work within our birth. Our masters require a pure, unblemished body, which is clothed with no stain nor any foreign mixture; for foreign addition is the leprosy of our metals.

The King's crown shall be of pure gold, and a chaste bride shall <insert>the</insert> to him

For no foreign waters must mix with our fountain, so that no monstrosity <note>original: "Mißgeburt"; in alchemical terms, a failed or "monstrous" chemical reaction</note> arises, and out of a healthy, wholesome fish, a snake does not crawl forth. Since also through intermediate ways a sharpness <note>original: "scherffe"; likely referring to the caustic acids used in the dissolution of metals</note> has been added, by which our body has been broken, so ensure that all corrosives <term>Corrosiu: acidic or caustic substances used to dissolve metals that must be washed away before the medicine is ingested</term> are washed away. When a tree brings forth no healthy, well-tasting fruit, it is cut off at its trunk, and another kind of better fruit is grafted <note>original: "geimpffet"</note> upon it. Then the scion <term>Reiß: a young shoot or twig used for grafting</term> unites with the trunk, so that from the trunk and its root, together with the scion, the whole becomes a good tree; and according to the desire of its grafter <note>original: "Impffer"</note>, it yields a good, healthy, and well-tasting fruit.

### Of the Great Stone

IN the courts of the great and powerful, many kinds of drinks are found, yet no two are alike in smell, color, or taste; for their preparation is varied, and nevertheless they are all consumed, because each is prepared for its specific place and is necessary for the maintenance of the court.

When the sun casts its rays and spreads them beneath the clouds, the common man says, "the sun is drawing water" <note>original: "die Sonne zeucht Wasser"; an old folk belief that visible sunbeams, or crepuscular rays, were the sun literally pulling up moisture to create rain</note> and it will rain; and if this occurs frequently, it promises a fruitful year. To build a precious and magnificent palace on high, many different kinds of masters and laborers must apply their hands and skills before the palace can be adorned and called complete; for that which is intended to be stone cannot be made of wood.

Through the daily ebbing <note>original: "Ablauff"; referring to the tide going out</note> of the raging sea and its return—which manifests and expresses itself out of an infused love <note>In alchemy, this "love" refers to the celestial influence or attraction between the stars and the earthly elements</note> received from the starry heavens above—there are many

## On the Great Stone
<note>The "Great Stone" refers to the "Philosopher's Stone," the legendary substance in alchemy capable of transmuting base metals into gold and granting eternal life.</note>

In the courts of the great and powerful, many kinds of drinks are found, and yet no two are alike in smell, color, and taste; for their preparation is manifold, and yet they are all drunk, since each is prepared in its own place and is necessary for the royal household <note>original: "Hofhalt"; the management, maintenance, or economy of a princely court</note>.

When the sun gives off and spreads its rays among the clouds, the common man says, "the sun is drawing water" <note>A traditional folk belief describing crepuscular rays—sunbeams that appear to radiate from the sun through gaps in clouds—as a sign that the sun is "sucking up" water to produce rain</note>, and it will rain; and if this happens often, it results in a fruitful year. To build a precious and magnificent palace on high, many and various kinds of masters and laborers must apply their hands and work, before the palace is adorned and can be called perfect; for that which should be made of stone cannot be made of wood.

Through the daily ebb and flow of the raging sea and its return—which acts and reveals itself through an infused love, such as it has received from above from the starry heavens—there are many
